On the Fourier spectrum of functions
on Boolean cubes

   A. Defant, M. Mastyło and A. Pérez

Abstract.

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   be a real-valued, degree-
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   Boolean function defined on the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional Boolean cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , and
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo>
   <mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></msub><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∏</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>
   S</mi></mrow></msub><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></mrow></mr
   ow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f(x)=\sum_{S\subset\{1,\ldots,d\}}\widehat
   {f}(S)\prod_{k\in S}x_{k}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   its Fourier-Walsh expansion. The main result states that there is an
   absolute constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that the
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
   mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo>/</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{2d/(d+1)}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   -sum of the Fourier coefficients of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow[-1,1]</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is bounded by
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><msup><mi>C</mi><msqrt>
   <mrow><mi>d</mi><mo lspace="0.167em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup></mrow><a
   nnotation encoding="application/x-tex">\leq C^{\sqrt{d\log
   d}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . It was recently proved that a similar result holds for complex-valued
   polynomials on the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional poly torus
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , but that in contrast to this a replacement of the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional torus
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   by
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[-1,1]^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   leads to a substantially weaker estimate. This in the Boolean case
   forces us to invent novel techniques which differ from the ones used in
   the complex or real case. We indicate how our result is linked with
   several questions in quantum information theory.
   ^†^†footnotetext: 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification: Primary
   06E30, 47A30. Secondary 81P45.^†^†footnotetext: Key words and phrases:
   Boolean functions, polynomial inequalities, Fourier analysis on
   groups.^†^†footnotetext: The second named author was supported by the
   National Science Centre, Poland, project no. 2015/17/B/ST1/00064. The
   research of the third author was partially done during a stay in
   Oldenburg (Germany) under the support of a PhD fellowship of “La Caixa
   Foundation”, and of the projects of MINECO/FEDER (MTM2014-57838-C2-1-P)
   and Fundación Séneca - Región de Murcia (19368/PI/14).

   .

1. Introduction

   The analysis of maps
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   defined on the Boolean cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is of interest in theoretical computer sciences, signal processing,
   social choice, combinatorics, graph theory, as well as others. For
   detailed explanation we refer to the surveys [22] and [26], or the more
   extensive book [21] and the references therein. The study of these
   functions is carried out through their polynomial expansion, a
   canonical representation of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in terms of basic functions known as Walsh functions, which are defined
   for every
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo>:=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S\subset[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}</annotation></
   semantics> :MATH]
   as
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi>S</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo rspace="0.170em"
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="1.017em">,</mo><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi>S</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>S</m
   i></msup><mo rspace="0.111em">:=</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">∏</mo><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>S</mi></mrow>
   </munder><msub><mi>x</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="1.017em">,</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo>
   </mrow><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">\chi_{S}:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{\pm 1\}\,,\hskip
   8.53581pt\chi_{S}(x)=x^{S}:=\prod_{k\in S}{x_{k}},\hskip
   8.53581ptx\in\{\pm 1\}^{n},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   where
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∅</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>:=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annot
   ation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\chi_{\emptyset}(x):=1</annotation></seman
   tics> :MATH]
   for each
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Using elementary linear algebra it can be checked that every
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   as above can be written as a unique linear combination of these
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi>S</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\chi_{S}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Another more profitable point of view to prove this fact is by means
   of Fourier analysis on groups: we can look at
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   as a compact abelian group endowed with the coordinatewise product and
   the discrete topology (the Haar measure is then given by the normalized
   counting measure). This way, for each function
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   we have an integral or expectation given by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>𝔼</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   maxsize="1.200em" minsize="1.200em">[</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   maxsize="1.200em"
   minsize="1.200em">]</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>:=</mo><mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn
   ><msup><mn>2</mn><mi>n</mi></msup></mfrac><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></munder><mrow><m
   i>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{E}\big[f\big]:=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\sum
   _{x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}{f(x)}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   The dual group of
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   actually consists of the set of all Walsh functions
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi>S</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\chi_{S}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S\subset[n]</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , which let us associate to each such
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   its Fourier-Walsh expansion
   (1)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">∑</mo><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></munder><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo lspace="0.170em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>S</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><mo rspace="1.017em">,</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo>
   </mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f(x)=\sum_{S\subset[n]}{\widehat{f}(S)\,x^
   {S}}\,,\hskip 8.53581ptx\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}\,,</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   where the coefficients are explicitly given by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mi>𝔼</mi><mo>⁡</m
   o><mrow><mo maxsize="1.200em"
   minsize="1.200em">[</mo><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.222em"
   rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><msub><mi>χ</mi><mi>S</mi></msub></mrow><mo
   maxsize="1.200em"
   minsize="1.200em">]</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\widehat{f}(S)=\mathbb{E}\big[f\cdot\chi_{
   S}\big]</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Thereby, a nonzero function
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   of degree
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   satisfies that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annota
   tion
   encoding="application/x-tex">\widehat{f}(S)=0</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   whenever
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>></mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|S|>d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , i.e., its Fourier spectrum concentrates on levels less than or equal
   to
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . We say that
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous if moreover
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annota
   tion
   encoding="application/x-tex">\widehat{f}(S)=0</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   provided
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≠</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">|S|\neq d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , i.e., the Fourier spectrum concentrates at level
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   .

1.1. Main result

   Blei in [6] proved that for each
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   there is a constant
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annota
   tion encoding="application/x-tex">C(d)>0</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   such that for each degree-
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   Boolean function
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f\colon\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   (2)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">∑</mo><mtable
   rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mrow>
   <mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></
   mtable></munder><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>S</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msup></mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</
   mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mfrac></msup><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mro
   w><mi>C</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo lspace="0.170em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></msub></mrow></mrow><mo
   >,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\subset[n]\
   \ |S|\leq
   d\end{subarray}}{|\widehat{f}(S)|^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}}\Big)^{\frac{d+1}{2d
   }}\leq C(d)\,\|f\|_{\{\pm 1\}^{n}},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   and here the exponent
   [MATH: <semantics><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\tfrac{2d}{d+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is best possible (to denote the supremum norm of a scalar-valued
   function on
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>K</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">K</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   we will often write
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>K</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|f\|_{K}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   instead of
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|f\|_{\infty}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   ). The best possible constant
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C(d)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is denoted by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo lspace="0.170em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\{\pm 1\}}^{\leq
   d}\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   The inequality (2) is a Boolean analog of an important inequality for
   complex polynomials due to Bohnenblust and Hille [7]. This inequality
   today forms a fundamental tool of the theory of Dirichlet series
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mi>n</mi></msub><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi
   ><mi>n</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>n</mi><mrow><mo>−</mo><mi>s</mi></mrow></m
   sup></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\sum_{n}a_{n}n^{-s}</annotation></semantic
   s> :MATH]
   , and it states that for every
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   there is a (best) constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   >≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   such for each complex valued degree-
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>z</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mtable
   rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><msubsup><mi>ℕ</mi
   ><mn>0</mn><mi>n</mi></msubsup></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mrow
   ><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></
   mtable></msub><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P(z)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\ma
   thbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\ |\alpha|\leq
   d\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   complex variables
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>z</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>z</mi><mi>n</mi></msub><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}</annotation></semantics
   > :MATH]
   (3)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">∑</mo><mtable
   rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><msubsup><mi>ℕ</mi
   ><mn>0</mn><mi>n</mi></msubsup></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mrow
   ><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></
   mtable></munder><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msup></mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</
   mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mfrac></msup><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msu
   bsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</m
   i></mrow></msubsup><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup></msub><
   /mrow></mrow><mo>;</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\ma
   thbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\ |\alpha|\leq
   d\end{subarray}}|a_{\alpha}|^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}\Big)^{\frac{d+1}{2d}}\leq
   \operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}\,\|f\|_{\mathbb{T}^{n}};</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   again the exponent
   [MATH: <semantics><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\tfrac{2d}{d+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   can not be improved. Originally, this result was proved for
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous polynomials only. If we define
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphant
   om><mo>=</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   lspace="0.170em">,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{=d}\,,</an
   notation></semantics> :MATH]

   in the by now obvious way, a straight forward trick shows that
   (4)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></
   mphantom><mo>=</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mi>BH
   </mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></
   msubsup></mrow><mo>;</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{=d}=\opera
   torname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq d}\,;</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   indeed, for a degree-
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>z</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><
   msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><annotation encoding="application/x-tex">P(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq
   d}a_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   on
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   the polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>Q</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>w</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><
   msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>w</mi><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>−</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow></msup></mrow></mrow></mrow><annot
   ation encoding="application/x-tex">Q(z,w)=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq
   d}a_{\alpha}z^{\alpha}w^{d-|\alpha|}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous on
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><
   /msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n+1}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   with the same set of coefficients and the same sup-norm.

   In recent years the Bohnenblust-Hille constants
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   >≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   were undertaken an intensive study. The original proof of (3) and its
   later improvements give upper estimates which are essentially of the
   order
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msup><msqrt><mi>d</mi></msqrt><mi>d</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\sqrt{d}^{d}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , but in [11] it was proved that
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   >≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   grows at most like
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msup><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt><mi>d</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\sqrt{2}^{d}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , and this was later substantially improved in [5], where the authors
   show that there is an absolute constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that
   (5)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></
   mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>≤</mo><msup><mi>C</mi
   ><msqrt><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo lspace="0.167em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup></mrow><m
   o lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}
   }^{\leq d}\leq C^{\sqrt{d\log d}}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   In this case we say that the growth of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   >≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is subexponential. In particular this implies that
   (6)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">lim sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></munder><mroot><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><
   mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsu
   p><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\
   sqrt[d]{\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{\leq
   d}}=1\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   A natural question appears related to the asymptotic decay of
   [MATH: <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\{\pm 1\}}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   as a function in
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   in the case when for each
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   the group
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is replaced by the group
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . Our main result is the following analog of (5).

Theorem 1.1.

   There is an absolute constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that for each
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d\in\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>≤</mo><msup><mi>C</mi><msqrt><mrow><mi>d</mi
   ><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup></mrow><m
   o lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\{\pm 1\}}^{\leq d}\leq
   C^{\sqrt{d\log{d}}}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   In particular,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mo>lim sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></msub><mroot><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mr
   ow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></m
   row><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt[d]{\oper
   atorname{BH}_{\{\pm 1\}}^{\leq d}}=1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   .

   In section 4.1 we indicate that this result is closely related with
   several questions in quantum information theory.

   Comparing Bohnenblust-Hille constants for complex polynomials on the
   polytorus
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   with Bohnenblust-Hille constants for real polynomials on the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[-1,1]^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   indicates that both behave substantially different. Therefore the
   preceding result for the Boolean cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   was not necessarily expected, and in fact its proof has to overcome new
   technical difficulties. We intend to explain all this in the rest of
   this introduction.

1.2. Real Bohnenblust-Hille constants

   A comparison of our proof of Theorem 1.1 with that of (5) shows that in
   fact in the setting of Boolean functions new substantial difficulties
   appear. Looking at (1) we see that each Boolean function
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f:\{\pm
   1\}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is the restriction a unique polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi>ℝ</mi></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{f}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}<
   /annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   being affine in each variable, a special type of real polynomial
   sometimes referred as tetrahedral:
   (7)
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\pm
   1\}^{n}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>ℝ</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,\,\mathbb{R}^{n}}
   </annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>ℝ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}}</annota
   tion></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><mi mathsize="0.700em">f</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
   f}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi mathsize="0.700em">P</mi><mi
   mathsize="0.710em">f</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
   P_{f}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   This identification is actually an isometry for the supremum norm since
   the fact that
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P_{f}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   is separately affine gives
   (8)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">:=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></munder><mrow><m
   o stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></munder><mrow><m
   o stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0em">sup</mo><mrow><mi>x</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></mrow></munder><mrow><m
   o stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mphantom></mphantom><mo
   lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub></mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\|f\|_{\infty}:=\sup_{x\in\{\pm
   1\}^{n}}{|f(x)|}=\sup_{x\in\{\pm
   1\}^{n}}{|P_{f}(x)|}=\sup_{x\in[-1,1]^{n}}{|P_{f}(x)|}=:\|P_{f}\|_{\inf
   ty}.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   We can then look at these objects from different points of view,
   allowing us to combine tools and techniques from Fourier analysis,
   polynomials and multilinear forms in order to study the Fourier
   spectrum of these functions. Based on results and ideas which have been
   developed in recent years in the setting of complex polynomials, this
   is indeed our aim in the present paper.

   What about the Bohnenblust-Hille inequality for real polynomials on the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional cube
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[-1,1]^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ? We denote by
   [MATH: <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{[-1,1]}^{\leq
   d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   the best constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C\geq 1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that for all real(!) degree-
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   polynomials
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>x</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mtable

   ><mn>0</mn><mi>n</mi></msubsup></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><mrow
   ><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mtd></mtr></
   mtable></msub><mrow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>α</mi></msup></mrow></mrow></mro
   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">P(x)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\ma
   thbb{N}_{0}^{n}\\ |\alpha|\leq
   d\end{subarray}}a_{\alpha}x^{\alpha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   we have
   (9)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">(</mo><mrow><mstyle displaystyle="true"><munder><mo

   ></mrow></mfrac></msup></mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</
   mn></mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mfrac></msup><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msu
   bsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo

   >,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c

   d\end{subarray}}|a_{\alpha}|^{\frac{2d}{d+1}}\Big)^{\frac{d+1}{2d}}\leq
   \operatorname{BH}_{[-1,1]}^{\leq

   and analogously we define for
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous polynomials the constant
   [MATH: <semantics><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo

   n></semantics> :MATH]
   (with its by now obvious meaning).

Proposition 1.2.

   We have
   (10)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">lim sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></munder><mroot><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><
   mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow><mo>=</mo><mn>2</mn></m
   row><mo lspace="0.170em">,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\
   sqrt[d]{\operatorname{BH}_{[-1,1]}^{=d}}=2\,,</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   and
   (11)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">lim sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></munder><mroot><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><
   mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mn>1</
   mn><mo>+</mo><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msqrt></mrow></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\
   sqrt[d]{\operatorname{BH}_{[-1,1]}^{\leq
   d}}=1+\sqrt{2}\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   The first equality is due to [10] whereas the second formula seems new.
   Interestingly, while the preceding proposition shows that for general
   real polynomials the situation is dramatically different from the
   complex case (6), the result from Theorem 1.1 indicates that
   tetrahedral polynomials seem to be halfway.

   In order to give a proof of Proposition 1.2,(11) we need some
   preliminary results. We collect a few crucial inequalities on
   polynomials which ’regulate the traffic’ between the sup norm of a real
   polynomial taken on
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[-1,1]^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   compared with the sup norm of its complexification taken on
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , as well as the relation of the sup norm of real or complex
   polynomials in relation to their homogeneous parts.

Lemma 1.3.

   Let
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>P</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo

   denote by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>P</mi><mi>m</mi></msub><mo

   pha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   [MATH: <semantics><mi>m</mi><annotation

     * (1)
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><mo

       encoding="application/x-tex">\|P_{m}\|_{\mathbb{T}^{n}}\leq\|P\|_{\
       mathbb{T}^{n}}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
        
     * (2)
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mrow><mo

       is best possible.

   Statement (1) is a standard Cauchy estimate. The second result is due
   to Klimek [16], and the third one due to Visser [25].

   Let us turn to the proof of statement (4): Denote by
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>d</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo

   w><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">T_{d}(x)=\sum_{m=0}^{d}{a_{m}(T_{d})}x^{k}
   ,\quad x\in\mathbb{R}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   Chebyshev’s polynomial of degree
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   [MATH:

   are given by

   [MATH:

   where
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo

   rt></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>d</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></msub></mrow></mrow></m
   row><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m=0}^{d}\operatorna
   me{BH}_{\mathbb{T}}^{=m}\,\,(1+\sqrt{2})^{d}\|P\|_{[-1,1]^{n}}</annotat
   ion></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mrow><msup><mi>C
   </mi><msqrt><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo lspace="0.167em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msq
   rt></mrow><mo stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>d</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>P</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup></msub></mrow></mrow><mo
   >,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\leq C^{\sqrt{d\log
   d}}d(1+\sqrt{2})^{d}\,\|P\|_{[-1,1]^{n}}\,,</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]

   which proves the upper estimate. Conversely, applying this inequality
   to Chebyshev’s polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>d</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">T_{d}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , we see that by (14)
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>+</mo><msqrt><mn>2</mn></msq
   rt></mrow><mo rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   lspace="0.1389em" movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">lim
   sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></munder><mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0.167em">sup</mo><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></m
   under><mroot><msub><mi>M</mi><mrow><mi>m</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow
   ></msub><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">≤</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0.167em">lim sup</mo><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">→</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow></munder><mroot><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><
   mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mi>d</mi></mroot></mrow></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">1+\sqrt{2}=\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\Bi
   g(\sup_{m\leq
   d}\sqrt[d]{M_{m,d}}\Big)\leq\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\sqrt[d]{\opera
   torname{BH}_{[-1,1]}^{\leq d}}\,,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   the conclusion. ∎

1.3. Sidon constants

   Let us compare (2) and (3) within the setting of so-called Sidon sets.
   Given
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>p</mi><mo>≤</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">1\leq p\leq\infty</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   and a compact abelian group
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>G</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">G</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , a finite subset
   [MATH: <semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Lambda</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   of the dual group
   [MATH: <semantics><mover
   accent="true"><mi>G</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\widehat{G}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is said to be a
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -Sidon set if there is a constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   (depending on
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>p</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">p</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><mi mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Lambda</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ) such that for every trigonometric polynomial
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo
   rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mo>∈</mo>
   <mi mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi></mrow></msub><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>γ</mi></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">f=\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}\widehat{f}(\gamm
   a)\gamma</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   we have
   (15)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msup><mrow><mo maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">∑</mo><mrow><mi>γ</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi></mrow></munder><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>f</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>γ</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mi>p</mi></msup></mrow><mo
   maxsize="1.600em"
   minsize="1.600em">)</mo></mrow><mfrac><mn>1</mn><mi>p</mi></mfrac></msu
   p><mo>≤</mo><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo lspace="0.170em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><msub><mrow><mo stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi>G</mi></msub></mrow></mrow><mo>,</mo>
   </mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Big(\sum_{\gamma\in\Lambda}{|\widehat{f}(
   \gamma)|^{p}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq
   C\,\|f\|_{G},</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   and the so-called Sidon constant
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><mo
   lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">(</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S_{p}(\Lambda)</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is the best possible
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>C</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Here we are mainly concerned with the groups
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional polytorus, and
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -dimensional Boolean cube. Recall that the dual group of
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   consists of all monomials
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">z^{\alpha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>ℤ</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><
   /mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}</annotation></sema
   ntics> :MATH]
   , whereas the dual group of
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   is formed by all monomials
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">x^{S}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ,
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S\subset[n]</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   . We concentrate on the following four sets of characters in the dual
   group of
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{T}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\{\pm 1\}^{n}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   , respectively:
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><msubsup><mi>ℕ</mi><mn>0</mn
   ><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo rspace="0.337em">,</mo><mo fence="false"
   rspace="0.167em" stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mo lspace="0.167em">=</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   rspace="0.680em"
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mtext>and</mtext><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0.278em"
   rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>α</mi><mo>∈</mo><msubsup><mi>ℕ</mi><mn>0</mn
   ><mi>n</mi></msubsup><mo rspace="0.337em">,</mo><mo fence="false"
   rspace="0.167em" stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mo lspace="0.167em">≤</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   rspace="0.170em"
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mo>,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Omega_{n}^{=d}=\big\{z^{\alpha}\colon\alp
   ha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n},\,|\alpha|=d\big\}\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\Om
   ega_{n}^{\leq
   d}=\big\{z^{\alpha}\colon\alpha\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n},\,|\alpha|\leq
   d\big\}\,,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   as well as
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.337em">,</mo><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mo lspace="0.167em">=</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   rspace="0.680em"
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mtext>and</mtext><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><msup><mi>x</mi><mi>S</mi></msup><mo
   lspace="0.278em" rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>n</mi><mo stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo
   rspace="0.337em">,</mo><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>S</mi><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mo lspace="0.167em">≤</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\Lambda_{n}^{=d}=\big\{x^{S}\colon
   S\subset[n],\,|S|=d\big\}\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\Lambda_{n}^{\leq
   d}=\big\{x^{S}\colon S\subset[n],\,|S|\leq
   d\big\}\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   In this terminology we have
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo
   lspace="0.1389em" movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0.167em">sup</mo><mi>n</mi></munder><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mfra
   c><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo><</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\operatorname{BH}_{\{\pm
   1\}}^{\leq d}=\sup_{n}S_{\frac{2d}{d+1}}(\Lambda_{n}^{\leq
   d})<\infty\,,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   and
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mi>𝕋</mi><mrow><mphantom></
   mphantom><mo>≤</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   rspace="0.1389em">=</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0.1389em"
   movablelimits="false"
   rspace="0.167em">sup</mo><mi>n</mi></munder><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mfra
   c><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><mo><</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></mrow><mo
   lspace="0.170em">,</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\displaystyle\operatorname{BH}_{\mathbb{T}
   }^{\leq d}=\sup_{n}S_{\frac{2d}{d+1}}(\Omega_{n}^{\leq
   d})<\infty\,,</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   both obvious reformulations of (2) and (3). Moreover, Theorem 1.1
   states that there exists a constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>≥</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C\geq 1</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that for all
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n,d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Λ</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>≤</mo><msup><mi>C</mi><msqrt><
   mrow><mi>d</mi><mo lspace="0.167em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup></mrow><m
   o>;</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\frac{2d}{d+1}}(\Lambda_{n}^{\leq
   d})\leq C^{\sqrt{d\log d}}\,;</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   we note again that by (5) the analog for
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>S</mi><mfrac><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
   lspace="0em"
   rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>+</mo><mn>1</mn
   ></mrow></mfrac></msub><mo lspace="0em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><msubsup><mi
   mathvariant="normal">Ω</mi><mi>n</mi><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>≤</
   mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S_{\frac{2d}{d+1}}(\Omega_{n}^{\leq
   d})</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   holds true.

2. The
[MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
-homogeneous case

   Let us come to the proof of Theorem 1.1, the Boolean analog of (5). We
   first prove the
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   -homogeneous case: There is an absolute constant
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>C</mi><mo>></mo><mn>0</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">C>0</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   such that for every
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>d</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">d\in\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   (16)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msubsup><mi>BH</mi><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
   lspace="0em">±</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow><mrow><mphantom></mphantom><mo>=</mo><mi>
   d</mi></mrow></msubsup><mo>≤</mo><msup><mi>C</mi><msqrt><mrow><mi>d</mi
   ><mo lspace="0.167em" rspace="0em">​</mo><mrow><mi>log</mi><mo
   lspace="0.167em">⁡</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></mrow></msqrt></msup></mrow><m
   o lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\operatorname{BH}_{\{\pm 1\}}^{=d}\leq
   C^{\sqrt{d\log{d}}}\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]

   Although Theorem 1.1 of course covers this case we prefer to give an
   independent proof since it is simpler and illustrates the general
   strategy. Anyway all proofs of Bohnenblust-Hille type inequalities –
   old ones or very recent ones – are very much inspired by the proof of
   the original estimate (3) which is itself a refinement of the proof of
   Littlewood’s famous
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mn>4</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>3</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">4/3</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   inequality from [18]. Let us recall its four crucial steps:
     * S1
       Given a
       [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       -homogeneous polynomial
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>z</mi><mo
       stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo
       rspace="0.111em">=</mo><mrow><msub><mo>∑</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">|</mo><mi>α</mi><mo
       stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mo>=</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow></msub><mr
       ow><msub><mi>a</mi><mi>α</mi></msub><mo lspace="0em"
       rspace="0em">​</mo><msup><mi>z</mi><mi>α</mi></msup></mrow></mrow><
       /mrow><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">f(z)=\sum_{|\alpha|=d}a_{\alpha}z^{\al
       pha}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       in
       [MATH: <semantics><mi>n</mi><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">n</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       variables, understand it as the restriction of a symmetric
       [MATH: <semantics><mi>d</mi><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">d</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       -linear form
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">L_{f}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       on
       [MATH: <semantics><msup><mi>ℂ</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{C}^{n}</annotation></semantics
       > :MATH]
       to the diagonal
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
       mathvariant="normal">Δ</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">{</mo><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>z</mi><mo>,</mo><mi
       mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>z</mi><mo rspace="0.278em"
       stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mo
       rspace="0.278em">:</mo><mrow><mi>z</mi><mo>∈</mo><msup><mi>ℂ</mi><m
       i>n</mi></msup></mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">}</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\Delta=\{(z,\ldots,z)\colon
       z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       .
     * S2
       Interpret
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">L_{f}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       as a symmetric matrix
       [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>=</mo><msubsup><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">(</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1<
       /mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
       mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>m</mi></ms
       ub></mrow></msub><mo
       stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msu
       b><mo>,</mo><mi
       mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>d</m
       i></msub><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></mrow><mi>n</mi></msubsup></mr
       ow><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">A=(c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}})_{i_{1},\ldo
       ts,i_{d}=1}^{n}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       , relate the
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
       mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
       lspace="0em"
       rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo>/</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo>
       +</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{2d/d+1}</annotation></semantics>
       :MATH]
       -norm of the coefficients of
       [MATH: <semantics><mi>f</mi><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">f</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       with the
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi
       mathvariant="normal">ℓ</mi><mrow><mrow><mrow><mn>2</mn><mo
       lspace="0em"
       rspace="0em">​</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo>/</mo><mi>d</mi></mrow><mo>
       +</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\ell_{2d/d+1}</annotation></semantics>
       :MATH]
       -norm of the entries of
       [MATH: <semantics><mi>A</mi><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">A</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       , and split this norm into mixed terms of the form

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><munder><mo
   movablelimits="false">∑</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>k</mi></msub></munder><
   msup><mrow><mo lspace="0em" maxsize="1.200em"
   minsize="1.200em">(</mo><mrow><munder><mo lspace="0em"
   movablelimits="false" rspace="0em">∑</mo><mtable
   rowspacing="0pt"><mtr><mtd><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,
   </mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mrow><mi>k</mi><m
   o>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow></msub></mrow></mtd></mtr><mtr><mtd><mrow><msu
   b><mi>i</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>d</mi></msub><
   /mrow></mtd></mtr></mtable></munder><msup><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo><msub><mi>c</mi><mrow><msub><mi>i</mi><mn>1</mn>
   </msub><mo>,</mo><mi
   mathvariant="normal">…</mi><mo>,</mo><msub><mi>i</mi><mi>d</mi></msub><
   /mrow></msub><mo
   stretchy="false">|</mo></mrow><mn>2</mn></msup></mrow><mo
   maxsize="1.200em"
   minsize="1.200em">)</mo></mrow><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>/</mo><mn>2</mn></mr
   ow></msup></mrow><mo lspace="0em">.</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\sum_{i_{k}}\big(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}
   i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1}\\
   i_{k},\ldots,i_{d}\end{subarray}}|c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}|^{2}\big)^{1/2
   }\,.</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
     * S3
       Use Khinchine’s inequality to estimate all of these mixed terms by
       the supremum norm of
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">L_{f}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
       on
       [MATH: <semantics><msup><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">(</mo><msup><mi>𝕋</mi><mi>n</mi></msup><mo
       stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow><mi>d</mi></msup><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">(\mathbb{T}^{n})^{d}</annotation></sem
       antics> :MATH]
       .
     * S4
       Finally, use polarization to estimate
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">‖</mo><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>f</mi></msub><mo
       stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi
       mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\|L_{f}\|_{\infty}</annotation></seman
       tics> :MATH]
       by
       [MATH: <semantics><msub><mrow><mo
       stretchy="false">‖</mo><mi>f</mi><mo
       stretchy="false">‖</mo></mrow><mi
       mathvariant="normal">∞</mi></msub><annotation
       encoding="application/x-tex">\|f\|_{\infty}</annotation></semantics
       > :MATH]
       .

   For the improvement of the constants, step-by-step non trivial
   refinements of the preceding arguments were needed. Modifying several
   arguments from [11] and [5] we now sketch the proof of (16), and start
   to collect a few crucial ingredients which will also be needed later
   for the more involved proof of Theorem 1.1.

2.1. Multilinear forms

   For each
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>n</mi><mo>∈</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">n\in\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   and finite set
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mi>ℕ</mi></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">A\subset\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics

   we define the sets
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi

   </mo><mi>𝐢</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"

   In case
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>A</mi><mo>=</mo><mrow><mo

   [MATH:

   :MATH]

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℐ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi

   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{J}(d,n)</annotation></semantics>

   repectively. We also consider the equivalence relation on
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi

   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{I}(A,n)</annotation></semantics>

   defined for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>𝐢</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>,</mo><mrow><msub

   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mi>𝐢</mi><msub><mrow></mrow><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∼</m

   Note that for each
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>𝐢</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi

   there is a unique
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>𝐣</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi

   such that
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>𝐣</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{j}\in[\mathbf{i}]</annotation></se
   mantics> :MATH]
   , where
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>𝐢</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[\mathbf{i}]</annotation></semantics>

   stands for the equivalent class of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>𝐢</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{i}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Moreover the number of elements of the equivalence class of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>𝐢</mi><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℐ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>A</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{i}\in\mathcal{I}(A,n)</annotation>
   </semantics> :MATH]
   is
   (17)
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo fence="false" rspace="0.167em"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>𝐢</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo fence="false"
   stretchy="false">|</mo><mo

   rspace="0.222em">⋅</mo><mrow><mrow><mo stretchy="false">|</mo><mrow><mo

   encoding="application/x-tex">|[\mathbf{i}]|=\frac{|A|!}{|\{k\colon\math
   bf{i}(k)=1\}|!\cdot\ldots\cdot|\{k\colon\mathbf{i}(k)=n\}|!}.</annotati
   on></semantics> :MATH]

   If
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>A</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">A_{1}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   and
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>A</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">A_{2}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   are two disjoint subsets of
   [MATH: <semantics><mi>ℕ</mi><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbb{N}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   , then the direct sum of
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>𝐢</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi

   otation></semantics> :MATH]

   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><msub><mi>𝐢</mi><mn>2</mn></msub><mo>∈</mo><mrow><mi

   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{i}_{2}\in\mathcal{I}(A_{2},n)</ann
   otation></semantics> :MATH]
   is defined as the map
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℐ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo

   which coincides with
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>𝐢</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathbf{i}_{k}</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   on
   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>A</mi><mi>k</mi></msub><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">A_{k}</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   for
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo>,</mo><
   mn>2</mn></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">k=1,2</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   . Note that every element of
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℐ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mrow><msub><mi>A</mi><mn>1</mn></msub><mo>∪</mo
   ><msub><mi>A</mi><mn>2</mn></msub></mrow><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">\mathcal{I}(A_{1}\cup
   A_{2},n)</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   can be represented this way. This yields in particular, that if
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>S</mi><mo>⊂</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">S\subset[d]</annotation></semantics>
   :MATH]
   and we denote
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mover
   accent="true"><mi>S</mi><mo>^</mo></mover><mo>:=</mo><mrow><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><mo>∖</mo><mi>S</mi></mrow></mrow><annota
   tion encoding="application/x-tex">\hat{S}:=[d]\setminus
   S</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   (its complement in
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mo stretchy="false">[</mo><mi>d</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">]</mo></mrow><annotation
   encoding="application/x-tex">[d]</annotation></semantics> :MATH]
   ), then
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><mrow><mi
   class="ltx_font_mathcaligraphic">ℐ</mi><mo>⁡</mo><mrow><mo
   stretchy="false">(</mo><mi>d</mi><mo>,</mo><mi>n</mi><mo
   stretchy="false">)</mo></mrow></mrow><mo>=</mo><mrow><mrow><mi

   The next result (sometimes called Blei’s inequality) appears with
   slightly different notation in [5, Theorem 2.1].

2.2. Hypercontractivity

   For each
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mn>1</mn><mo>≤</mo><mi>p</mi><mo><</mo><mi

   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>L</mi><mi>p</mi></msub><annotation

   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mi>f</mi><mo lspace="0.278em"

   is given by
   [MATH: <semantics><mrow><mrow><msub><mrow><mo

   A very important map is the noise operator defined for each
   [MATH:
   <semantics><mrow><mrow><mo>−</mo><mn>1</mn></mrow><mo><</mo><mi>ρ</mi><

   [MATH: <semantics><msub><mi>T</mi><mi>ρ</mi></msub><annotation
